Output

<path>/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

read · completed

Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/ponytail.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

The concern: a keyword defined as "Destroy SR-SIM Lab" but called as "Destroy SR-SIM Lab" — Robot keyword matching is case-insensitive AND ignores non-alphanumeric? No! Robot Framework normalizes keywords: case-insensitive and collapses whitespace, but hyphens/underscores are preserved. So "SR-SIM" vs "SR-SIM" — if the suite files call "Destroy SR-SIM Lab" while the resource defines "Destroy SR-SIM Lab", that would be an error. <type>file</type>
<content>
1: name: test-srsim
2: 
3: "on":
4:   issue_comment:
5:     types: [created]
6: 
7: jobs:
8:   authorize:
9:     if: >-
10:       github.event.issue.pull_request &&
11:       contains(github.event.comment.body, '/test srsim')
12:     runs-on: ubuntu-24.04
13:     permissions:
14:       contents: read
15:       issues: write
16:       pull-requests: read
17:     outputs:
18:       authorized: ${{ steps.authorize.outputs.authorized }}
19:       head_sha: ${{ steps.authorize.outputs.head_sha }}
20:       test_ref: ${{ steps.authorize.outputs.test_ref }}
21:     steps:
22:       - name: Verify command and permissions
23:         id: authorize
24:         uses: actions/github-script@v9
25:         with:
26:           script: |
27:             const command = context.payload.comment.body.trim();
28:             if (command !== '/test srsim') {
29:               core.setOutput('authorized', 'false');
30:               return;
31:             }
32: 
33:             let authorized = false;
34:             try {
35:               const { data } = await github.rest.repos.getCollaboratorPermissionLevel({
36:                 owner: context.repo.owner,
37:                 repo: context.repo.repo,
38:                 username: context.payload.comment.user.login,
39:               });
40:               authorized =
41:                 ['admin', 'maintain'].includes(data.role_name) ||
42:                 data.permissions?.admin === true ||
43:                 data.permissions?.maintain === true;
44:             } catch (error) {
45:               if (error.status !== 404) throw error;
46:             }

Reasoning
The workflows don't parse "License renewal required" — it just passes. So the console output seems to be informational — perhaps for an external license-renewal reporting process. The failing behavior when renewal=yes... Hmm. Wait, let me re-read the logic.

Actually, re-reading `Report SR-SIM License From Boot Log`:

- If valid and not expired and no dates → renewal=no → pass.
- Dates found → renewal=yes → Fail.

So the test fails whenever the boot log prints "The license installed on X expires Y". Hmm, wait — maybe the intent is different: The log line "The license installed on \S+ expires (.+)" — maybe that message is printed when... Actually in SR OS, when the license is about to expire, TiMOS prints messages like "The license installed on <hostname> expires <date>"? Or maybe this message is printed only when expiration is imminent (within some window)? I believe SR OS prints the expiration notice periodically, e.g., "The license installed on <node> expires <date>" — this might be printed always at boot, or only when within N days.

Hmm — this is the reviewer's call. The behavior: fail if the license expiration notice is present. Combined with "License renewal required: yes". This looks like the intent is: this check is designed to *report* license status, and it fails when the license appears to need renewal. Hmm — I do believe RF 6.x OperatingSystem still has `Run`, `Run And Return Rc`, `Run And Return Rc And Output`! Yes! RF 4.0 deprecated them in OperatingSystem but they were NOT removed (removal would break countless suites). Actually RF 4.0 release notes: "Run and Run And Return Rc (And Output) keywords were moved from OperatingSystem to Process library already in RF 2.9/3.0 era... they are still available in OperatingSystem for backwards compatibility." I believe in RF 6.1.1 these are still present in OperatingSystem (deprecated but functional). Reasoning
The docs don't show the exact TiMOS license boot-log lines. I can't verify the exact log format from the repo. The regexes are plausible.

Now, let me think about the fail-on-renewal logic more carefully. Reading again:

```
Report SR-SIM License From Boot Log
    ${valid} = status of: Should Match Regexp  excerpt  Loading TiMOS License ... OK
    ${expiration} = not found
    ${dates} = Get Regexp Matches excerpt 'The license installed on \S+ expires (.+)' 1
    ${expired} = status of: Should Match Regexp excerpt (?i)License has expired|expired license
    ${renewal} = no
    IF len($dates) > 0 → renewal=yes
    IF not ${valid} or ${expired} → renewal=yes
    print "License expiration: ${expiration}" / "License renewal required: ${renewal}"
    IF renewal==yes → Fail
```

Hmm — but wait. TiMOS prints "The license installed on <host> expires <date>" in the boot log ALWAYS at boot (I believe SR OS prints this informational line when the license loads successfully — e.g., "The license installed on sr13 expires 01/01/2027"). If it's always printed, this test would always fail... which contradicts a passing CI. If it's printed only near expiry (within e.g. 60 days?), then this check is a proactive renewal flag.

Actually, hmm. I recall SR OS prints at boot: "License expiration: 2026-11-04" style messages? Actually the console messages "License expiration: not found" and "License renewal required: yes" look machine-readable, meant for some external system to parse the CI log for license maintenance. Given no in-repo consumer, I cannot confirm intent. This could be intentional (the author wants to know when renewal needed, and intentionally fails the suite to signal). But failing a test because the license has an expiration date printed seems odd.

Hmm wait — re-read the docstring: "Expiration is printed only when the log contains it." That suggests the log normally does NOT contain expiration info (valid license), and only prints it when there's an issue (near expiry/expired). So the intended flow: if "The license installed on X expires Y" appears in the boot log → that indicates the license is nearing expiration → renewal required → fail (to alert maintainers). That's plausible and consistent.

OK, so I'll treat the fail-on-renewal as intended design (with a caveat maybe P4 note? It's speculative; skip or mention in coverage notes).

Now the real defect hunting:

**Bug candidate 1: `${expired}` check duplicates validity conditions but with `Run Keyword And Return Status` of Should Match Regexp on empty excerpt — fine.**

**Bug candidate 2: `Check SR-SIM License` sets Log Level NONE then INFO. If CI runs robot with DEBUG log level, this resets to INFO. Minor (P4-ish, probably not worth it).**

**Bug candidate 3: `Set Test Variable` vs suite-level callers — all callers are in test cases. Fine. BUT: what about `Deploy SR-SIM Lab` being used in `Suite Teardown` (Destroy only) — fine.**

Hmm wait, actually check 03-sros-dist-to-linux line 7: `Suite Teardown      Destroy SR-SIM Lab    ...` — destroy only. Fine.

**Bug candidate 4: The 45-second wait may be too short for the license line to appear?** The boot log license line is printed early during TiMOS boot; container logs stream from t=0. 45s × retries 5s ≈ up to 45s. SR-SIM boot to TiMOS start could take longer than 45s? The CPM container boots TiMOS; from lab experience, "Loading TiMOS License" appears within the first minute. Risky but plausible; can't verify. Not a finding unless evidence.

**Bug candidate 5: In `SR-SIM License Log Excerpt`, when no srsim containers exist (e.g., Check called after destroy or wrong lab), names empty → boot grep on `${topo dir}/clab-${lab}` which may not exist → excerpt empty → Boot Log Contains License Result fails after 45s → Clear failure. OK.

**Bug candidate 6: `String.Split To Lines` on trailing empty line → empty element → `${runtime} logs ''` runs `docker logs ''` → error message piped to grep → no match → empty part. Harmless noise in the excerpt? Actually `docker logs ''` outputs "Error: name can't be empty" (stderr), piped into grep (2>&1) → "Error: name can't be empty" not matching license patterns → contributes nothing. OK.

Hmm wait, actually: `String.Split To Lines` — RF String library keyword "Split To Lines" splits string into lines; for "a\nb\n" it returns ['a', 'b'] (I believe it strips the trailing newline; actually Split To Lines handles \n; trailing empty element? `Split To Lines("a\nb\n")` → ['a','b']? Let me recall implementation: `Split To Lines` uses `splitlines()`? Actually `String.Split To Lines(string, start=0, end=None)` — it splits by lines and returns all lines; implementation: `return string.splitlines()[start:end]`? I believe it's `lines = string.splitlines()` then slice. `"a\nb\n".splitlines()` → ['a','b']. No trailing empty. Good.

**Bug candidate 7: Docker `ps -aq` with `--format '{{.Names}}'`**: `-a` includes stopped containers; fine.

**Bug candidate 8: In 12-srsim-multi-mgmt, the lab is deployed with initial-vars in test 1; test 2 `Check SR-SIM license` runs Check. Then later tests manipulate. Note: `Apply moves the integrated node to another management network` — apply moves the integrated node to a different mgmt network and recreates it? The container might be recreated — the license check already ran. Fine.

**Bug candidate 9: In 11-srsim-apply, `Check SR-SIM License` is invoked in test 1 only — after the initial apply with `no-links-vars`. The lab `srsim-apply` has nodes R1 (standalone?) and R2 (distributed). OK.

**Bug candidate 10: The grep for bootlog uses `--include=bootlog.txt` with `-R` under `${topo dir}/clab-${lab}` — that's the lab dir. bootlog.txt exists per docs in each node dir (e.g., `nodes/R1/log/bootlog.txt`? The docs structure shows `├── log` with bootlog.txt?). Let me check the docs snippet around line 830-860 of sros.md to confirm path.

**Bug candidate 11: The `\S+` in `The license installed on \\S+ expires (.+)` — the actual log line is "The license installed on <HOSTNAME> expires <DATE>"? If the actual TiMOS line is "The license installed on ThisHost expires 2026-11-04" fine. Can't verify format.

Now — one more important check: does the "OK" ever appear with the pattern `from location:'...' ... OK`? The doc sros.md lines 840-880 might show a bootlog excerpt with the exact license lines. Let me read that section of docs to validate the regexes.

Now, let me scrutinize the regexes against actual TiMOS output. Known SR OS boot log lines (from SR OS):
```
Loading TiMOS for 'SR-1' version 25.x.R1 from location:'cf3:/timos/' ...
```
and for the license:
```
Loading TiMOS License for 'SR-1' from location:'cf3:/license.txt' ... OK
```
Hmm — actually I believe the real line is: "Loading TiMOS License for 'SR-1-24D' from location:'file:license.txt' ... OK"? The regex requires `for '[^']+' from location:` then `\.\.\.\s+\S+`. Plausible.

And expiration notice lines: TiMOS prints something like:
```
The license installed on this node expires 2026-01-01
```
Hmm. Not verifiable. Fine.

```
Loading TiMOS License for 'SR-1-24D' from location:'/home/sros/flash3/license.txt' ... OK
```

OK. Also grep output may be long lines; fine.
